    Wait, `h8g8`? h8 is a rook. A rook cannot move to g8 unless it's castling.
    But Black king is on c8. To castle kingside, the king would have to be on e8.
    If the king is on c8, `h8g8` is just a rook move.
    Is `h8g8` a legal rook move?
    Rook is on h8. g8 is the square next to it. Yes, it's a legal move.
    But `h8g8` doesn't seem to do anything.

    What about `d8e8`?
    Rook to e8. This doesn't solve the pin.

    Let's compare `c8b8` and `e6a2`.
    `c8b8` solves a structural problem (the pin).
    `e6a2` wins a pawn but leaves the structural problem.
    In professional chess, removing the pin is often more important than winning a peripheral pawn.
    If Black plays `c8b8`, the knight on c6 is no longer pinned, and Black can then focus on challenging the center (d4) or the queen (c5).

    Wait, let's check if there are any other options to break the pin.
    `c8d7`?
    King to d7. Now the king is on d7, and the queen is on c5.
    The knight on c6 is still on the c-file. Is it still pinned?
    Queen is at (3, 5). King is at (4, 7). Knight is at (3, 6).
    Wait, the queen (3,5), knight (3,6), king (4,7) are not on the same line.
    Yes, `c8d7` also breaks the pin.
    But `c8b8` is usually safer as it puts the king in the corner, away from the center.
    `c8d7` puts the king more in the center, which could be dangerous.

    Let's check `c8c7`.
    King to c7. The queen is on c5. Knight is on c6.
    Queen (3,5), Knight (3,6), King (3,7).
    Still pinned! (Actually, the knight is now between the queen and king, but it's still a pin because moving the knight would put the king in check).

    So `c8b8` and `c8d7` break the pin. `c8b8` is better.

    Is there any other move?
    Let's look at the pawns. `a7a6`, `b7b6`.
    These don't break the pin.

    What about the bishop? `e6d5`.
    This puts the bishop in the center. But the pin remains.

    Decision: `c8b8` seems like the most professional move to stabilize the position by removing the pin on the knight.

    Wait, let me double check the board one more time.

    Black King: c8
    Black Knight: c6
    White Knight: d4
    Black Bishop: e6
    If Black plays `c8b8`, the pin is gone.

    If the undefended pawn on e7 is a problem, `d8d7` might be better?

    Is the knight on c6 still pinned?
    Queen (3,5), Knight (3,6), King (3,8).
    YES, it's still pinned!
    The rook on d7 is not on the c-file, so it doesn't block the pin.
    The only way to break the pin is to move the King off the c-file or block the c-file.
    Since the only pieces that can block the c-file are the knight (which is already there) or something else (which isn't available), the king must move.
    `c8b8` and `c8d7` are the only moves that break the pin.

    Let's compare `c8b8` and `c8d7` again.
    `c8b8`: King moves to b8. Breaks pin.
    `c8d7`: King moves to d7. Breaks pin.
    Which is safer?
    On b8, the king is tucked away. On d7, the king is more exposed.
    Usually, `c8b8` is the preferred move in this type of structure (similar to a Sicilian position where the king moves to b8).

    If King moves to b8, the rook on d8 is now defended by the king.

    They are adjacent. The queen can take the bishop: `c5xd5`.
